3.5
Optimal sample sizes

When discussing the notion of optimal sample sizes we must first begin with a definition of what is optimal. Typically, experimenters wish to achieve the smallest possible uncertainty with the fewest number of total observations. This goal is manifested in the following definition.

Definition 11

(Optimal sample sizes)
.

Let
\mathbf{X}_{j}=(X_{i,j},\dots,X_{n_{j},j})
with
X_{i,j}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}F_{j}(\theta_{j})
denote a random sample of size
n_{j}
drawn from the distribution
F_{j}
with parameters
\theta_{j}
. Furthermore, let
T(\mathbf{X})
denote an estimator based on
M
random samples
\mathbf{X}=(\mathbf{X}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{X}_{M})
and
\mathsf{ACV}T(n|\theta_{T})
denote its absolute coefficient of variation as a function of the sample sizes
n=(n_{1},\dots,n_{M})
with parameters
\theta_{T}=\cup_{j}\theta_{j}
. Then the optimal sample sizes are defined as the vector-valued function
n^{\text{opt}}(\mathrm{N},\theta_{T})=(n_{1}^{\text{opt}},\dots,n_{M}^{\text{opt}})(\mathrm{N},\theta_{T})
that minimizes
\mathsf{ACV}T(n|\theta_{T})
subject to
\mathrm{N}=n_{1}+\cdots+n_{M}
for some fixed
\mathrm{N}>0
.

In the case where there are two samples, so that
n=(n_{1},n_{2})
, this definition of optimality requires solving the single-variable optimization problem

\displaystyle n_{2}^{\text{opt}}(\mathrm{N},\theta_{T})

\displaystyle=\arginf_{n_{2}\in[0,\mathrm{N}]}\mathsf{ACV}^{2}T(\mathrm{N}-n_{2},n_{2}|\theta_{T})

(3.11a)

\displaystyle n_{1}^{\text{opt}}(\mathrm{N},\theta_{T})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{N}-n_{2}^{\text{opt}}(\mathrm{N},\theta_{T}),

(3.11b)

for some total number of samples
\mathrm{N}
and parameters
\theta_{T}
. Note that while this formulation does give the optimal sample sizes according to definition
11
, it does not allow one to specify a predetermined value (desired outcome) for
\mathsf{ACV}T
. Since it may be desirable to specify the outcome of
\mathsf{ACV}T
instead of
\mathrm{N}
we can introduce the constraint
\mathsf{ACV}T(n_{1}^{\text{opt}},n_{2}^{\text{opt}}|\theta_{T})=\mathsf{acv}_{0}
and reformulate (
3.11
) into the equivalent two-variable problem

\displaystyle\inf_{n_{2}\in[0,\mathrm{N}]}

\displaystyle\mathsf{ACV}^{2}T(\mathrm{N}-n_{2},n_{2}|\theta_{T})\big|_{(\mathrm{N},n_{2})=(n_{1}^{\text{opt}}+n_{2}^{\text{opt}},n_{2}^{\text{opt}})}

(3.12a)

\displaystyle\mathsf{ACV}^{2}T(n_{1}^{\text{opt}},n_{2}^{\text{opt}}|\theta_{T})-\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{2}=0,

(3.12b)

which can then be solved for
(n_{1}^{\text{opt}},n_{2}^{\text{opt}})
in terms of
\mathsf{acv}_{0}
and
\theta_{T}
.

Before proceeding, let’s consider what this system of equations says and how we solve it. Three cases must be considered which we shall denote as:
nondegenerate
(
n_{1}^{\text{opt}},n_{2}^{\text{opt}}\neq 0
),
weakly degenerate
(
n_{1}^{\text{opt}}=0
or
n_{2}^{\text{opt}}=0
but not both), and
degenerate
(
n_{1}^{\text{opt}},n_{2}^{\text{opt}}=0
). For the nondegenerate case we begin with equation (
3.12a
) by fixing the total number of samples to
\mathrm{N}
and then minimizing
\mathsf{ACV}^{2}T
in
n_{2}
subject to this constraint. This process gives the optimal sample size
n_{2}^{\text{opt}}
in terms of
\mathrm{N}
and
\theta_{T}
4
4

4

In fact, if we were to stop here equation (
3.12a
) would be identical to (
3.11a
)
, which upon substituting
\mathrm{N}=n_{1}^{\text{opt}}+n_{2}^{\text{opt}}
yields an equation representing the optimal relationship, a.k.a. the
optimality relation
, between the two sample sizes. Solving the optimality relation for either
n_{1}^{\text{opt}}
or
n_{2}^{\text{opt}}
and then substituting into (
3.12b
) we then obtain one of the optimal sample sizes parameterized in terms of
\mathsf{acv}_{0}
and
\theta_{T}
. This solution may then be used in conjunction with the optimality relation to obtain the solution for the remaining optimal sample size. In the case of weak degeneracy, the infimum of
\mathsf{ACV}^{2}T(\mathrm{N}-n_{2},n_{2}|\theta_{T})
occurs at the boundary
n_{2}=0,\mathrm{N}
and so our two-sample problem reduces to that of finding a single optimal sample size via (
3.12b
). Lastly, the case of degeneracy results when
\mathsf{ACV}T\to 0
and so zero samples are needed to achieve the desired outcome
\mathsf{ACV}T=\mathsf{acv}_{0}
. As will be seen in the latter analysis, this situation can occur at boundary points of the parameter space and thus be treated as a limit of the nondegenerate case.

3.5.1
Asymptotic properties at low illumination

One of the unanswered curiosities of Section
3.4
was how the quantity
\mathscr{E}
behaves at low illumination w.r.t. the parameters
(\sigma_{\mathrm{d}},g,n_{1},n_{2})
. If we fix these parameters and consider what only happens as the illumination decreases, i.e. as
\zeta\searrow 1
, we see that
\mathsf{CV}\bar{P}\to\infty
while
\mathsf{CV}\mathscr{T}_{\nu}
approaches a constant and thus
\mathscr{E}\to 0
. From this observation one might conclude that the only way to achieve
\mathscr{E}
-values near unity is to perform measurements at high illumination; thus, extinguishing the possibility of approximating confidence intervals and optimal sample sizes for
\mathscr{G}_{\nu}
with those of
\mathscr{T}_{\nu}
under low illumination conditions. However, if we instead consider
\mathscr{E}
as a function of the optimal samples sizes for
\mathscr{T}_{\nu}
or
\mathscr{G}_{\nu}
, which vary with the illumination level, the limiting behavior at low illumination changes drastically. The following analysis takes a look at the behavior of the optimal sample sizes for
\bar{P}
,
\mathscr{T}_{\nu}
, and
\mathscr{G}_{\nu}
under low illumination conditions. The main results are found in Lemma
29
, Theorem
17
, and Corollary
14
, which show for each estimator that the optimal samples sizes are asymptotically equal and proportional to
(1-\zeta)^{-2}
as
\zeta\searrow 1
. We will then conclude this section by using these asymptotic results in Corollary
15
to study how
\mathscr{E}
behaves at low illumination when subject to optimal sample sizes.

Lemma 29
.

Let
(n_{1}^{{\text{opt}}},n_{2}^{{\text{opt}}})
denote the optimal sample sizes for
\bar{P}
that also satisfy
\mathsf{ACV}\bar{P}(n_{1}^{{\text{opt}}},n_{2}^{{\text{opt}}}|\zeta,\sigma_{\mathrm{d}},g)=\mathsf{acv}_{0}
for some fixed
\mathsf{acv}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Then as illumination decreases,
\zeta\searrow 1
,
n_{2}^{{\text{opt}}}/n_{1}^{{\text{opt}}}\to 1
, and
n_{i}^{{\text{opt}}}\sim C_{\bar{P}}(1-\zeta)^{-2}
where
C_{\bar{P}}=2((\sigma_{\mathrm{d}}g)^{2}\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{2})^{-1}
.

Proof.

For the sake of brevity, we begin by letting
a=\frac{1}{(\sigma_{\mathrm{d}}g)^{2}}\frac{\zeta}{(1-\zeta)^{2}}
and writing

\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\bar{P}(n_{1},n_{2}|\zeta,\sigma_{\mathrm{d}},g)=a\left(\frac{1}{n_{1}}+\frac{\zeta}{n_{2}}\right).

If
\zeta\in(0,1)
, then
a\neq 0
and
\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\bar{P}(\mathrm{N}-n_{2},n_{2}|\theta_{\bar{P}})
is smooth and strictly convex in
n_{2}
on
(0,\mathrm{N})
. Additionally, the limiting value of
\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\bar{P}(\mathrm{N}-n_{2},n_{2}|\theta_{\bar{P}})
at the endpoints
n_{2}=0,\mathrm{N}
is infinite and so we know our optimization problem is nondegenerate. These observations lead us to conclude that
(\ref{eq:optimal_SOEs})
can be uniquely solved by

\displaystyle\partial_{n_{2}}\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\bar{P}(\mathrm{N}-n_{2},n_{2}|\theta_{\bar{P}})\Big|_{(\mathrm{N},n_{2})=(n_{1}^{\text{opt}}+n_{2}^{\text{opt}},n_{2}^{\text{opt}})}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\bar{P}(n_{1}^{\text{opt}},n_{2}^{\text{opt}}|\theta_{\bar{P}})-\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{2}

\displaystyle=0,

which upon substituting appropriate values produces the system of equations

\displaystyle(1)

\displaystyle a\left(\frac{1}{(n_{1}^{\text{opt}})^{2}}-\frac{\zeta}{(n_{2}^{\text{opt}})^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle(2)

\displaystyle a\left(\frac{1}{n_{1}^{\text{opt}}}+\frac{\zeta}{n_{2}^{\text{opt}}}\right)-\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{2}

\displaystyle=0.

Upon inspection,
(1)
gives us the optimality relation
n_{2}^{\text{opt}}/n_{1}^{\text{opt}}=\sqrt{\zeta}
and so as
\zeta\searrow 1
we have
n_{2}^{\text{opt}}/n_{1}^{\text{opt}}\to 1
which is the first claim. Substituting the optimality relation into
(2)
and solving for
n_{2}^{\text{opt}}
further yields
n_{2}^{\text{opt}}=a/\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{2}(\sqrt{\zeta}+\zeta)
. Making use of this result and again calling on the optimality relation we have after reintroducing
a
:

\displaystyle n_{1}^{\text{opt}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(\sigma_{\mathrm{d}}g)^{2}\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{2}}\frac{\zeta(1+\sqrt{\zeta})}{(1-\zeta)^{2}}

\displaystyle n_{2}^{\text{opt}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(\sigma_{\mathrm{d}}g)^{2}\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{2}}\frac{\zeta(\sqrt{\zeta}+\zeta)}{(1-\zeta)^{2}}.

Now as
\zeta\searrow 1
,
\zeta(1+\sqrt{\zeta})\sim\zeta(\sqrt{\zeta}+\zeta)\sim 2+\mathcal{O}(1-\zeta)
; hence,

n_{i}^{\text{opt}}\sim\frac{2}{(\sigma_{\mathrm{d}}g)^{2}\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{2}}(1-\zeta)^{-2}+\mathcal{O}((1-\zeta)^{-1}),\quad i=1,2

which proves the second claim. Recall that we assumed
\zeta\in(0,1)
so as to render our optimization problem nondegenerate. However, one can obtain the optimal sample sample sizes at the endpoints
\zeta=1
and
\zeta=0
by considering the appropriate limits, the latter of which gives us the degenerate case
(n_{1}^{\text{opt}},n_{2}^{\text{opt}})=(0,0)
. The proof is now complete.
∎

In Lemma
29
we studied the optimal sample sizes for
\bar{P}
that satisfied Definition
11
and the constraint
\mathsf{ACV}\bar{P}=\mathsf{acv}_{0}
. Unlike
\bar{P}
, the estimator
\mathscr{T}_{\nu}
is biased so we will investigate its optimal sample sizes w.r.t. a desired outcome for both
\mathsf{ACV}\mathscr{T}_{\nu}
and
\mathsf{ARB}\mathscr{T}_{\nu}
. We could also choose
\mathsf{ARB}\mathscr{T}_{\nu}=\mathsf{arb}_{0}
to be constant but this may not always be desirable as will be seen in later sections. In particular, notice that for any
\nu>0
,
\mathsf{ARB}\mathscr{T}_{\nu}=\zeta^{\nu}\to 0
in the shot noise limit
\zeta\nearrow 0
. As such, forcing a bias on
\mathscr{T}_{\nu}
in the shot noise limit by requiring
\mathsf{ARB}\mathscr{T}_{\nu}=\mathsf{arb}_{0}
could be viewed as counterproductive in certain contexts. These observations steer us in the direction of specifying a constraint on
\mathsf{ARB}\mathscr{T}_{\nu}
that varies with illumination level and vanishes in the shot noise limit. To help aid in our discussion we introduce the notion of the
bias profile
.

Definition 12

(Bias profile)
.

Let
T\sim F_{T}(\theta_{T})
be an estimator of
g(\theta_{T})\neq 0
for which
\mathsf{ARB}T=\rho(\theta_{T})
. Then we say
\rho(\theta_{T})
is the bias profile for
T
.

Given the simplicity of the expression for
\mathsf{ARB}\mathscr{T}_{\nu}
we can choose just about any bias profile we desire by setting
\nu=\log_{\zeta}\rho
. With so many available options we will aim for simplicity and proceed with the following.

Definition 13
.

For
\zeta\in[0,1]
,
\mathsf{arb}_{0}\in(0,1)
, and
b\in\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
,
{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}\coloneqq\log_{\zeta}\mathsf{arb}_{0}+b
.

Proposition 7
.

Let
{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
be as defined in Definition
13
. Then, the bias profile of
\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
is
\rho=\mathsf{arb}_{0}\zeta^{b}
.

This choice of bias profile is useful since it is simple and guarantees
\mathsf{ARB}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}\leq\mathsf{arb}_{0}
everywhere on
\zeta\in[0,1]
. Furthermore, the parameter
b
provides the flexibility to choose a constant bias profile
(b=0)
or a profile that vanishes to zero in the shot noise limit
(b>0)
. With this matter out of the way we present Lemmas
30
-
32
, which provide us with the necessary results for studying the behavior of the optimal sample sizes of
\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
under low-illumination conditions in Theorem
17
.

Definition 14

(Lower-incomplete gamma function)
.

For
\Re s>0

\gamma(s,z)\coloneqq\int_{0}^{z}t^{s-1}e^{-t}\,\mathrm{d}t.

Lemma 30
.

Let
{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
be as define in Definition
13
. Then as
\zeta\searrow 1

\tilde{g}_{n,\omega}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})\sim\frac{\gamma(n+1,-\log\mathsf{arb}_{0})}{1-\mathsf{arb}_{0}}(1-\zeta)^{-n}+\mathcal{O}((1-\zeta)^{1-n}),

where
\gamma(s,z)
is the lower-incomplete gamma function of Definition
14
.

Proof.

From Corollary
10
form
(\mathrm{iii})
we have

(1-\zeta)^{n}\tilde{g}_{n,\omega}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})=n!\frac{\zeta^{n-\omega}-\mathsf{arb}_{0}\zeta^{b}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{(\log_{\zeta}\mathsf{arb}_{0}+b-n+\omega)_{k}}{k!}(1-\zeta)^{k}}{1-\mathsf{arb}_{0}\zeta^{b}}.

As
\zeta\searrow 1
, one may use the generalized binomial theorem to deduce
\zeta^{x}\sim 1+\mathcal{O}(1-\zeta)
. Furthermore, we also determine as
\zeta\searrow 1
:
\log_{\zeta}\mathsf{arb}_{0}\to\infty
so that
(\log_{\zeta}\mathsf{arb}_{0}+b-n+\omega)_{k}\sim\log_{\zeta}^{k}\mathsf{arb}_{0}
and
[(1-\zeta)\log_{\zeta}\mathsf{arb}_{0}]^{k}\sim(-\log\mathsf{arb}_{0})^{k}(1+\mathcal{O}(1-\zeta))
. After making the appropriate substitutions and collecting terms we find

(1-\zeta)^{n}\tilde{g}_{n,\omega}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})\sim\Gamma(n+1)\frac{1-\mathsf{arb}_{0}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{(-\log\mathsf{arb}_{0})^{k}}{k!}}{1-\mathsf{arb}_{0}}+\mathcal{O}(1-\zeta),

which according to
[
27
, Eq.
06.06.03.0009.01
]
can be written in terms of the lower incomplete gamma function. Solving for
\tilde{g}_{n,\omega}
completes the proof.
∎

Definition 15

(Polygamma function)
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

\psi^{(n)}(z)\coloneqq\partial_{z}^{n+1}\log\Gamma(z).

Lemma 31
.

Let
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
and

f_{k}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\frac{1}{(\alpha_{1})_{\ell}(\alpha_{2})_{k-\ell}\ell!(k-\ell)!},\quad k\in\mathbb{N}.

Then for all
k
and some constant
\mathrm{A}>0
, the optimal pair
(\alpha_{1}^{\text{opt}},\alpha_{2}^{\text{opt}})
that minimize
f_{k}
subject to
\mathrm{A}=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}
is

(\alpha_{1}^{\text{opt}},\alpha_{2}^{\text{opt}})=(\tfrac{1}{2}\mathrm{A},\tfrac{1}{2}\mathrm{A}).

Proof.

Working with the properties of the Pochhammer symbol and Relation
12
we may write
f_{k}
in the equivalent form

f_{k}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=\frac{1}{k!}\frac{(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}-1+k)_{k}}{(\alpha_{1})_{k}(\alpha_{2})_{k}}.

Substituting
\mathrm{A}=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}
and differentiating w.r.t.
\alpha_{2}
yields

\partial_{\alpha_{2}}f_{k}(\mathrm{A}-\alpha_{2},\alpha_{2})=f_{k}(\mathrm{A}-\alpha_{2},\alpha_{2})\Psi_{k}^{(0)}(\mathrm{A}-\alpha_{2},\alpha_{2}),

where

\Psi_{k}^{(0)}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=\psi^{(0)}(\alpha_{1}+k)-\psi^{(0)}(\alpha_{1})-\psi^{(0)}(\alpha_{2}+k)+\psi^{(0)}(\alpha_{2})

and
\psi^{(n)}(z)
is the polygamma function of Definition
15
. By repeated application of the recurrence relation
\psi^{(n)}(z+1)=\psi^{(n)}(z)+(-1)^{n}n!z^{-n-1}
we deduce

\Psi_{k}^{(0)}(\alpha_{2})=\sum_{m=0}^{k-1}\left(\frac{1}{\mathrm{A}-\alpha_{2}+m}-\frac{1}{\alpha_{2}+m}\right)\begin{cases}<0,&0<\alpha_{2}<\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{A}\\
=0,&\alpha_{2}=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{A}\\
>0,&\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{A}<\alpha_{2}<\mathrm{A}.\end{cases}

Further noting that
f_{k}(\mathrm{A}-\alpha_{2},\alpha_{2})
is positive proves it has a unique global minimum at
\alpha_{2}=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{A}
, which completes the proof.
∎

Definition 16

(Exponential integral function)
.

\operatorname{Ei}(z)\coloneqq\int_{0}^{z}\frac{e^{t}-1}{t}\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{2}\left(\log z-\log\left(\tfrac{1}{z}\right)\right)+\gamma,

where
\gamma=0.577216\dots
is the Euler-Mascheroni constant.

Lemma 32
.

For
c,x\in\mathbb{R}

\int_{[0,x]^{2}}e^{-u+cuv-v}\,\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v=\tfrac{1}{c}e^{-1/c}\left(\operatorname{Ei}\left(\tfrac{1}{c}\right)-2\operatorname{Ei}\left(\tfrac{1}{c}(1-cx)\right)+\operatorname{Ei}\left(\tfrac{1}{c}(1-cx)^{2}\right)\right),

where
\operatorname{Ei}(z)
is the exponential integral function of Definition
16
.

Proof.

Let
I
denote the integral in question. Integrating w.r.t.
u
and then substituting
t=(cv-1)x
yields

I=\tfrac{1}{c}e^{-1/c}\int_{-x}^{x(cx-1)}e^{-t/(cx)}\frac{e^{t}-1}{t}\,\mathrm{d}t.

With a bit of algebra we may further write

I=\tfrac{1}{c}e^{-1/c}\left(\int_{-x}^{x(cx-1)}\frac{e^{(1-1/(cx))t}-1}{t}\,\mathrm{d}t-\int_{-x}^{x(cx-1)}\frac{e^{-1/(cx)t}-1}{t}\,\mathrm{d}t\right).

Now substituting
s=(1-1/(cx))t
and
s=-1/(cx)t
into the first and second integral, respectively, we have after some simplification

I=\tfrac{1}{c}e^{-1/c}\left(\int_{0}^{1/c}-2\int_{0}^{(1-cx)/c}+\int_{0}^{(1-cx)^{2}/c}\right)\frac{e^{s}-1}{s}\,\mathrm{d}s.

From Definition
16
, if
z\in\mathbb{R}
then
\int_{0}^{z}\frac{1}{t}(e^{t}-1)\,\mathrm{d}t=\operatorname{Ei}(z)-\log|z|-\gamma
. Substituting this result into
I
, all of the logarithmic and constant terms cancel leaving us with the desired result.
∎

Theorem 17
.

Let
(n_{1}^{{\text{opt}}},n_{2}^{{\text{opt}}})
denote the optimal sample sizes for
\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
that also satisfy
\mathsf{ACV}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}(n_{1}^{{\text{opt}}},n_{2}^{{\text{opt}}}|\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})=\mathsf{acv}_{0}
and
\mathsf{ARB}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}=\mathsf{arb}_{0}\zeta^{b}
for
\mathsf{acv}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
\mathsf{arb}_{0}\in(0,1)
, and
b\in\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
fixed. Then as illumination decreases,
\zeta\searrow 1
,
n_{2}^{{\text{opt}}}/n_{1}^{{\text{opt}}}\to 1
, and
n_{i}^{{\text{opt}}}\sim C_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}(1-\zeta)^{-2}
where
C_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}
is the solution to

\overline{\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}(C_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}},\mathsf{arb}_{0})-\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{2}=0

with

\overline{\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}=\frac{(C_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}/4)e^{-C_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}/4}}{(1-\mathsf{arb}_{0})^{2}}\biggl(\operatorname{Ei}\left(\tfrac{C_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}}{4}\right)-2\operatorname{Ei}\left(\log\mathsf{arb}_{0}+\tfrac{C_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}}{4}\right)\\
+\operatorname{Ei}\left(\tfrac{(C_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}/4+\log\mathsf{arb}_{0})^{2}}{C_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}/4}\right)\biggr)-1

and
\operatorname{Ei}(z)
is the exponential integral function of Definition
16
.

Proof.

First note that
\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
satisfies the constraint
\mathsf{ARB}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}=\mathsf{arb}_{0}\zeta^{b}
independent of
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
. Using the result of Lemma
30
and the relationship
\alpha_{i}=(n_{i}-1)/2
we deduce as
\zeta\searrow 1

\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}(n_{1},n_{2}|\zeta,\mathsf{arb}_{0},b)\sim\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{f_{k}\left(\tfrac{n_{1}-1}{2},\tfrac{n_{2}-1}{2}\right)}{(1-\zeta)^{2k}}(a_{k}^{2}(\mathsf{arb}_{0})+\mathcal{O}(1-\zeta)),

where
f_{k}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
is as defined in Lemma
31
and
a_{k}(\mathsf{arb}_{0})=\gamma(k+1,-\log\mathsf{arb}_{0})/(1-\mathsf{arb}_{0})
. By Lemma
31
we know that the optimal sample sizes
(n_{1}^{\text{opt}},n_{2}^{\text{opt}})
that minimizes
f_{k}
with
\mathrm{N}=n_{1}+n_{2}
fixed occurs when
n_{1}=n_{2}
; thus,
n_{2}^{{\text{opt}}}/n_{1}^{{\text{opt}}}\to 1
as
\zeta\searrow 1
which is our first claim. Letting
n_{1}^{{\text{opt}}}=n_{2}^{{\text{opt}}}=n^{{\text{opt}}}
we have

\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}(n_{1}^{\text{opt}},n_{2}^{\text{opt}}|\theta_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}})\sim\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{f_{k}\left(\tfrac{n^{\text{opt}}-1}{2},\tfrac{n^{\text{opt}}-1}{2}\right)}{(1-\zeta)^{2k}}(a_{k}^{2}(\mathsf{arb}_{0})+\mathcal{O}(1-\zeta)),

Since
(1-\zeta)^{-2k}
becomes arbitrarily large in the neighborhood of
\zeta=1
the function
f_{k}
must simultaneously become arbitrarily small as to maintain a finite value of
\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
in the limit. But
f_{k}((n^{\text{opt}}-1)/2,(n^{\text{opt}}-1)/2)
is a decreasing function of
n^{\text{opt}}
so it must be that
n^{\text{opt}}\to\infty
as
\zeta\searrow 1
. Making use of the asymptotic relation
(s)_{k}\sim s^{k}(1+\mathcal{O}(s^{-1}))
as
s\to\infty
and binomial theorem we deduce for
n^{\text{opt}}\to\infty
:

f_{k}\left(\tfrac{n^{\text{opt}}-1}{2},\tfrac{n^{\text{opt}}-1}{2}\right)\sim\frac{1}{k!}\left(\frac{4}{n^{\text{opt}}}\right)^{k}+\mathcal{O}((n^{\text{opt}})^{-k-1}),

which upon substitution into our expression for
\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
gives

\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}(n_{1}^{\text{opt}},n_{2}^{\text{opt}}|\theta_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}})\sim\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{k!}\left(\frac{4}{(1-\zeta)^{2}n^{\text{opt}}}\right)^{k}+\frac{\mathcal{O}((n^{\text{opt}})^{-k-1})}{(1-\zeta)^{2k}}\right)\\
\cdots\times(a_{k}^{2}(\mathsf{arb}_{0})+\mathcal{O}(1-\zeta)).

Given that we require
\mathsf{ACV}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}=\mathsf{acv}_{0}<\infty
in the limit it follows that we must have
n^{\text{opt}}\sim C_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}(1-\zeta)^{-2}+\mathcal{O}((1-\zeta)^{-1})
for some positive constant
C_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}
which proves our second claim. Substituting this asymptotic form for
n^{\text{opt}}
into our expression for
\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
we see that all error terms are
\mathcal{O}(1-\zeta)
so that upon passing to the limit we obtain

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\zeta\searrow 1\\
\mathsf{ACV}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}=\mathsf{acv}_{0}\\
\mathsf{ARB}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}=\mathsf{arb}_{0}\zeta^{b}\end{subarray}}\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}(n_{1}^{\text{opt}},n_{2}^{\text{opt}}|\theta_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}})=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\gamma^{2}(k+1,-\log\mathsf{arb}_{0})}{(1-\mathsf{arb}_{0})^{2}}\frac{\left(4/C_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}\right)^{k}}{k!},

where
C_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}
is the positive constant that solves

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\gamma^{2}(k+1,-\log\mathsf{arb}_{0})}{(1-\mathsf{arb}_{0})^{2}}\frac{\left(4/C_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}\right)^{k}}{k!}=\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{2}.

(3.13)

Letting
\overline{\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}
denote the series on the l.h.s. of (
3.13
) we use the integral representation of the lower incomplete gamma function in Definition
14
to write

\overline{\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{[0,-\log\mathsf{arb}_{0}]^{2}}\frac{e^{-(u+v)}}{(1-\mathsf{arb}_{0})^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{\left(4uv/C_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}\right)^{k}}{k!}\,\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v.

The integrand is the sum of nonnegative terms and is bounded above by its limiting form as
n\to\infty
which in turn is integrable on the domain of integration; thus, by argument of dominated convergence we have

\overline{\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}=\int_{[0,-\log\mathsf{arb}_{0}]^{2}}\frac{e^{-u+4uv/C_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}-v}}{(1-\mathsf{arb}_{0})^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v-1,

which is evaluated in terms of the exponential integral function with Lemma
32
. The proof is now complete.
∎

Figure
3.1
plots
C_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}
on the unit square. These values for
C_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}
were computed in
mathematica
using a Newton-Raphson iteration with starting point

C_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}^{\ast}=\frac{2\gamma^{2}(2,-\log\mathsf{arb}_{0})}{\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{2}(1-\mathsf{arb}_{0})^{2}}\left(1+\left(1+2\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{2}(1-\mathsf{arb}_{0})^{2}\frac{\gamma^{2}(3,-\log\mathsf{arb}_{0})}{\gamma^{4}(2,-\log\mathsf{arb}_{0})}\right)^{1/2}\right),

which is the solution to
(\ref{eq:CT_solution_series_form})
using only the
k=1,2
terms.

Figure 3.1:
Plot of
C_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}(\mathsf{arb}_{0},\mathsf{acv}_{0})
on the unit square.

Since
\mathsf{ARB}\mathscr{G}_{\nu}=\mathsf{ARB}\mathscr{T}_{\nu}
we may combine the results of Lemma
29
and Theorem
17
to make the following claims about the optimal sample sizes for
\mathscr{G}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
at low illumination.

Corollary 14
.

Let
(n_{1}^{{\text{opt}}},n_{2}^{{\text{opt}}})
denote the optimal sample sizes for
\mathscr{G}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
that also satisfy
\mathsf{ACV}\mathscr{G}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}(n_{1}^{{\text{opt}}},n_{2}^{{\text{opt}}}|\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}},\sigma_{\mathrm{d}},g)=\mathsf{acv}_{0}
and
\mathsf{ARB}\mathscr{G}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}=\mathsf{arb}_{0}\zeta^{b}
for
\mathsf{acv}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
\mathsf{arb}_{0}\in(0,1)
, and
b\in\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
fixed. Then as illumination decreases,
\zeta\searrow 1
,
n_{2}^{{\text{opt}}}/n_{1}^{{\text{opt}}}\to 1
, and
n_{i}^{{\text{opt}}}\sim C_{\mathscr{G}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}(1-\zeta)^{-2}
where
C_{\mathscr{G}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}
is the solution to

\overline{\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{G}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}(C_{\mathscr{G}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}},\mathsf{arb}_{0},\sigma_{\mathrm{d}},g)-\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{2}=0

with

\overline{\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{G}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}=\overline{\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}(C_{\mathscr{G}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}},\mathsf{arb}_{0})+2\frac{\overline{\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}(C_{\mathscr{G}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}},\mathsf{arb}_{0})}{(\sigma_{\mathrm{d}}g)^{2}C_{\mathscr{G}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}}+\frac{2}{(\sigma_{\mathrm{d}}g)^{2}C_{\mathscr{G}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}}

and
\overline{\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}
given in Theorem
17
.

Proof.

Begin by writing

\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{G}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}=\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}+(\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})(\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\bar{P})+\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\bar{P}.

As was the case in Theorem
17
,
\mathscr{G}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
satisfies the constraint on
\mathsf{ARB}\mathscr{G}_{\nu}
independent of
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
. Furthermore, from Lemma
29
and Theorem
17
we know in the limit
\zeta\searrow 1
that the optimal sample sizes for
\bar{P}
and
\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
are asymptotically equal and proportional to
(1-\zeta)^{-2}
. Substituting
n_{1}^{\text{opt}}=n_{2}^{\text{opt}}\sim C_{\mathscr{G}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}(1-\zeta)^{-2}
and passing to the limit gives the desired expression for
\overline{\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{G}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}
.
∎

We are finally able to return to the questions surrounding the behavior of
\mathscr{E}
as a function of the optimal sample sizes at low illumination.

Corollary 15
.

Let
T
stand in place for
\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
or
\mathscr{G}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
and
(n_{1}^{\text{opt}},n_{2}^{\text{opt}})
denote the optimal sample sizes for
T
such that
\mathsf{ACV}T=\mathsf{acv}_{0}
and
\mathsf{ARB}T=\mathsf{arb}_{0}\zeta^{b}
with
\mathsf{acv}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
\mathsf{arb}_{0}\in(0,1)
, and
b\in\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
fixed. Furthermore, define
\mathscr{E}_{T}
to be the quantity
\mathscr{E}
as a function of
(n_{1}^{\text{opt}},n_{2}^{\text{opt}})
and
\overline{\mathscr{E}_{T}}=\lim_{\zeta\searrow 1}\mathscr{E}_{T}
. Then,

\overline{\mathscr{E}_{T}}=\left(1+(1+\overline{\mathsf{ACV}^{-2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}(C_{T},\mathsf{arb}_{0}))\frac{2}{(\sigma_{\mathrm{d}}g)^{2}C_{T}}\right)^{-1},

where
\overline{\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}
is given in Theorem
17
. In particular, if
T=\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
:

\mathscr{E}_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}=\left(1+\frac{1+\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{-2}}{(\sigma_{\mathrm{d}}g)^{2}}\frac{\zeta}{(1-\zeta)^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{n_{1}^{\text{opt}}}+\frac{\zeta}{n_{2}^{\text{opt}}}\right)\right)^{-1}

and

\overline{\mathscr{E}_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}}=\left(1+\frac{2(1+\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{-2})}{(\sigma_{\mathrm{d}}g)^{2}C_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}}\right)^{-1}.

Corollary
15
shows us that when one substitutes the optimal sample sizes for
\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
or
\mathscr{G}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
into
\mathscr{E}
, the limit
\lim_{\zeta\searrow 1}\mathscr{E}_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}
is nonzero. Additionally, for the special case where the optimal sample sizes for
\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
are chosen, both
\mathscr{E}_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}
and
\overline{\mathscr{E}_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}}
reduce to very simple and easy to compute expressions. To get a sense for how
\overline{\mathscr{E}_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}}
behaves w.r.t. its parameters, Table
3.3
presents numerical values of this quantity for select values of
\sigma_{\mathrm{d}}g
,
\mathsf{arb}_{0}
, and
\mathsf{acv}_{0}
. One can see for the chosen parameter values that the dark noise, denoted
\sigma_{\mathrm{d}}g
, plays a significant role in the proximity of
\overline{\mathscr{E}_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}}
to one. Indeed, for
\sigma_{\mathrm{d}}g
greater than about
5\,e\mathchar 45\relax
we observe
\overline{\mathscr{E}_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}}\approx 1
, which implies that the optimal sample sizes for
\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
and
\mathscr{G}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
are nearly identical even at near zero illumination.

Using the optimal sample sizes of
\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
in place of those for
\mathscr{G}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
is not just convenient for producing a compact expression for
\mathscr{E}
. When measuring
g
in an actual experiment, one cannot know the optimal sample sizes with certainty because they are based on unknown parameters. For
\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
, the optimal sample sizes are a function of
\zeta=\sigma_{\mathrm{d}}^{2}/\sigma_{\mathrm{p}+\mathrm{d}}^{2}
, which is itself a function of two unknown parameters that must be estimated. This problem is compounded when considering optimal sample sizes for
\mathscr{G}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
which are a function of
(\sigma_{\mathrm{d}}^{2},g,\zeta)
and thus require estimating four parameters, i.e.
(\mu_{\mathrm{d}},\mu_{\mathrm{p}+\mathrm{d}},\sigma_{\mathrm{d}},\sigma_{\mathrm{p}+\mathrm{d}})
. The two additional degrees of freedom render estimating the optimal sample sizes for
\mathscr{G}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
in a real experiment impractical due to the amount of uncertainty introduced in the process of estimation. Instead, it is far better to measure
g
in a region where
\mathscr{E}\approx 1
so that the optimal sample sizes for
\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
can be substituted. As has been demonstrated here, this situation is possible even when the illumination level is near zero small provided a sufficiently large dark noise.

.

3.5.2
Computation of optimal sample sizes for
\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}

Given that the optimal samples sizes for
\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
are most desirable for experimentation, this section is dedicated to further studying their characteristics as well as computing them. Our approach will be organized as follows. We will begin by using the analysis in Section
2.4.1
to derive a few results pertaining to zeros and special values of
\tilde{g}_{n,\omega}(z,\nu)
evaluated at
(z,\nu)=(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})
. These results will then allow us to write the system of equations needed to solve for the optimal sample sizes
(n_{1}^{\text{opt}},n_{2}^{\text{opt}})
as well as provide exact solutions at two points of interest. From here we will derive explicit approximations for the optimal sample sizes and use our previous findings to determine the usefulness of these approximations. Finally, the derived approximations will be used as the starting point in numerical methods to compute
(n_{1}^{\text{opt}},n_{2}^{\text{opt}})
, which will be demonstrated for some sample values.

The following proposition defines some notation needed to discuss the zeros of
\tilde{g}_{n,\omega}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})
in Lemma
33
.

Proposition 8
.

Let
{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}(\zeta)=\log_{\zeta}\mathsf{arb}_{0}+b
be a function of
\zeta
as defined in Definition
13
,
\mathbb{N}_{b}\coloneqq\{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:n\geq b\}
,
\zeta_{b,n}\coloneqq\sqrt[n-b]{\mathsf{arb}_{0}}\mathds{1}_{b<n}
, and
Z_{b}\coloneqq\{\zeta_{b,n}:n\in\mathbb{N}_{b}\}
. Then,

(1)

{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}(\zeta)
is strictly increasing from
[0,1]
onto
[b,\infty)
,

(2)

{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}[Z_{b}]=\mathbb{N}_{b}
.

Lemma 33

(Zeros of
\tilde{g}_{n,\omega}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})
)
.

\tilde{g}_{n,\omega}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})=0\iff(n,n-\omega)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}\land(\zeta=0\lor(\zeta\in Z_{b}\setminus\{0\}\land n-\omega\geq{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})).

Proof.

The proof follows from Lemma
17
which states

\tilde{g}_{n,\omega}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})=0\iff A\lor B,

with
A=(n\in\mathbb{N}\land n>\omega\land{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}\in\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}\land z=0)
and
B=(n-\omega-{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\land{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}\neq 0)
. Starting with
A
notice that
{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}\in\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
always holds so that we may equivalently write
A=((n,n-\omega)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}\land\zeta=0)
. Next, consider
B
and observe that a necessary condition for
{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}=0
is
\zeta=0
. Since the condition
\zeta=0
is already covered by
A
we may write
B=(n-\omega-{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\land\zeta\neq 0)
. Furthermore,
n-\omega-{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
requires
{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
to be an integer and
n-\omega\geq{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
. But
{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
can only be an integer if
\zeta\in Z_{b}
so that
B=(n-\omega\geq{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}\land\zeta\in Z_{b}\setminus\{0\})
. But the conditions specified by
B
implicitly require
n-\omega\geq 1\implies(n,n-\omega)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
so that this requirement can be factored out of
A
and
B
leaving us with the desired result.
∎

Corollary 16
.

For all
(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})\in[0,1]\times[b,\infty)

n=\omega\implies\tilde{g}_{n,\omega}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})\neq 0.

Furthermore,

\tilde{g}_{n,\omega}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})=0,\ \forall(n,n-\omega)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}\iff\zeta\in\{0,\zeta_{b,1}\}.

Proof.

The first claim immediately follows from Lemma
33
since
n=\omega\implies n-\omega=0\notin\mathbb{N}
. Furthermore, the conditions of Lemma
33
are always satisfied if
(n,n-\omega)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
and
\zeta=0\lor{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}=1
, where the latter holds if and only if
\zeta\in\{0,\zeta_{b,1}\}
.
∎

Corollary
16
will turn out to be very important in determining the behavior of optimal sample sizes of
\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
. In particular, the results of this corollary indicate that
\zeta=0
and
\zeta=\zeta_{b,1}
are the only values of
\zeta
for which
\tilde{g}_{n,\omega}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})=0
for all
n>\omega
. We shall see that these two points of interest corresponds to the only
\zeta
-values that render the optimization problem for the optimal sample sizes of
\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
weakly degenerate. Before proceeding, we need one more result, which gives us the expression for
\tilde{g}_{n,n}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})
at
\zeta=0,\zeta_{b,1}
.

Lemma 34
.

Let
L\in\{0,\zeta_{b,1}\}
. Then
\lim_{\zeta\nearrow L}\tilde{g}_{n,n}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})=n!
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Proof.

From Corollary
10
form
(\mathrm{iii})
we have for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
:

\tilde{g}_{n,n}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})=n!\frac{1-\mathsf{arb}_{0}\zeta^{b}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{({\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})_{k}}{k!}(1-\zeta)^{k}}{(1-\zeta)^{n}(1-\mathsf{arb}_{0}\zeta^{b})}.

Each case is now examined separately.

(1)

\zeta\nearrow 0
.

Proof.

If
b>0
then
\zeta\nearrow 0\implies\zeta^{b}\nearrow 0
and so
\tilde{g}_{n,n}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}_{b>0})\to n!
. Likewise, if
b=0
then
\zeta^{b}=1
while
({\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})_{k}\to\mathds{1}_{k=0}
and so again we find
\tilde{g}_{n,n}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}_{b=0})\to n!
as expected.
∎

(2)

\zeta\nearrow\zeta_{b,1}
.

Proof.

If
b\geq 1
then
\zeta_{b,1}=0
, which was already covered in part
(1)
. Now suppose
0\leq b\leq 1
. As
\zeta\nearrow\zeta_{b,1}
:
{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}\to 1
and
\mathsf{arb}_{0}\zeta^{b}\to\zeta_{b,1}
; hence

\lim_{\zeta\nearrow\zeta_{b,1}}\tilde{g}_{n,n}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}_{b\in[0,1]})=n!\frac{1-\zeta_{b,1}\sum_{k=0}^{n}(1-\zeta_{b,1})^{k}}{(1-\zeta_{b,1})^{n+1}}=n!,

which completes the proof.
∎

∎

Our next task is to derive the system of equations needed for computing the optimal sample sizes. Since
\mathsf{ACV}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
is naturally parameterized in terms of the shape parameters
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
and not the sample sizes
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
our discussion will focus on finding the optimal shape parameters according to Definition
11
, knowing that we may convert these optimal shape parameters to their corresponding sample sizes through the relation
n_{i}^{\text{opt}}=2\alpha_{i}^{\text{opt}}+1
. As was done in Lemma
29
we will accomplish this task by letting
\mathrm{A}=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}
be constant and then showing that
\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu}(\mathrm{A}-\alpha_{2},\alpha_{2})
is:
(1)
twice continuously differentiable and strictly convex on
\alpha_{2}\in(0,\mathrm{A})
and
(2)
is infinite at the endpoints
\alpha_{2}=0,\mathrm{A}
with the exception of two points of interest. These findings will then be used to show that the minimization in
(\ref{eq:optimal_SOEs})
can be uniquely solved by equating a derivative with zero. The following result presents and intermediate step in demonstrating convexity of
\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu}(\mathrm{A}-\alpha_{2},\alpha_{2})
.

Lemma 35
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathrm{A}>0
,
\zeta\in[0,1]
, and
{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
be as defined in Definition
13
. Then,

f_{k}(\alpha_{2}|\mathrm{A},\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}):=\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\frac{\tilde{g}_{k,\ell}^{2}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})}{(\mathrm{A}-\alpha_{2})_{\ell}(\alpha_{2})_{k-\ell}\ell!(k-\ell)!}

is positive and strictly convex on
\alpha_{2}\in(0,\mathrm{A})
. Furthermore,

\lim_{\alpha_{2}\nearrow 0}f_{k}(\alpha_{2}|\cdot)=\begin{cases}k!/(\mathrm{A})_{k},&\zeta\in\{0,\zeta_{b,1}\}\\
\infty,&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

and

\lim_{\alpha_{2}\searrow\mathrm{A}}f_{k}(\alpha_{2}|\cdot)=\infty.

Proof.

The claim of positivity comes from the fact that the summand of
f_{k}
is nonnegative and from Corollary
16
which implies
\tilde{g}_{n,\omega}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})\neq 0
for at least one
\ell\in\{0,\dots,k\}
. For the claim of convexity we first write

\partial_{\alpha_{2}}^{2}\frac{1}{(\mathrm{A}-\alpha_{2})_{\ell}(\alpha_{2})_{k-\ell}}=\frac{[\Psi^{(0)}_{k,\ell}(\mathrm{A}-\alpha_{2},\alpha_{2})]^{2}+\Psi^{(1)}_{k,\ell}(\mathrm{A}-\alpha_{2},\alpha_{2})}{(\mathrm{A}-\alpha_{2})_{\ell}(\alpha_{2})_{k-\ell}},

where

\displaystyle\Psi^{(0)}_{k,\ell}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})

\displaystyle=\psi^{(0)}(\alpha_{1})-\psi^{(0)}(\alpha_{1}+\ell)-\psi^{(0)}(\alpha_{2})+\psi^{(0)}(\alpha_{2}+k-\ell)

\displaystyle\Psi^{(1)}_{k,\ell}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})

\displaystyle=\psi^{(1)}(\alpha_{1})-\psi^{(1)}(\alpha_{1}+\ell)+\psi^{(1)}(\alpha_{2})-\psi^{(1)}(\alpha_{2}+k-\ell).

Certainly,
[\Psi^{(0)}_{k,\ell}(\mathrm{A}-\alpha_{2},\alpha_{2})]^{2}\geq 0
for all
k
,
\ell
, and
\alpha_{2}\in(0,\mathrm{A})
. Furthermore,

\Psi^{(1)}_{k,\ell}(\mathrm{A}-\alpha_{2},\alpha_{2})=\sum_{j=0}^{\ell-1}\frac{1}{(\mathrm{A}-\alpha_{2}+j)^{2}}+\sum_{j=0}^{k-\ell-1}\frac{1}{(\alpha_{2}+j)^{2}}.

Since
(k,\ell)\in(\mathbb{N},\mathbb{N}_{0})
with
\ell\leq k
, it follows that at least one of these sums will be nonzero such that
\Psi^{(1)}_{k,\ell}(\mathrm{A}-\alpha_{2},\alpha_{2})>0
and
[(\mathrm{A}-\alpha_{2})_{\ell}(\alpha_{2})_{k-\ell}]^{-1}
is a strictly convex function of
\alpha_{2}
on
(0,\mathrm{A})
. Combining this observation with the fact that there is always at least one
\ell\in\{0,\dots,k\}
for which
\tilde{g}_{k,\ell}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})\neq 0
further implies that
f_{k}
is a finite sum of at least one positive strictly convex function with the rest of the terms being either being zero or also positive and strictly convex; hence,
f_{k}
is positive and strictly convex on
\alpha_{2}\in(0,\mathrm{A})
.

Now turning to the limits notice that if
\zeta\in\{0,\zeta_{b,1}\}
then by Corollary
16
and Lemma
34
we have
f_{k}(\alpha_{2}|\cdot)=\tilde{g}_{k,k}^{2}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})/((\mathrm{A}-\alpha_{2})_{k}k!)\to k!/(\mathrm{A})_{k}
as
\alpha_{2}\nearrow 0
. For the complementary case
\zeta\in[0,1]\setminus\{0,\zeta_{b,1}\}
we may use the the asymptotic expansion
\frac{1}{(x)_{m}}\sim\frac{1}{\Gamma(m)}(\frac{1}{x}-H_{m-1}+\mathcal{O}(x))
for
x\to 0
and Lemma
34
to further deduce

f_{k}(\alpha_{2}|\cdot)\sim\frac{k!}{(\mathrm{A})_{k}}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{2}^{-1})\to\infty

as
\alpha_{2}\nearrow 0
. For the other limit
\alpha_{2}\searrow\mathrm{A}
, Corollary
16
tells us that the
\ell=0
term in
f_{k}
is always positive; hence, as
\alpha_{2}\searrow\mathrm{A}

f_{k}(\alpha_{2}|\cdot)\sim\mathcal{O}((\mathrm{A}-\alpha_{2})^{-1})\to\infty,

which completes the proof.
∎

Theorem 18

(Optimal sample sizes of
\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
)
.

Let
(n_{1}^{{\text{opt}}},n_{2}^{{\text{opt}}})(\theta_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}})
with
\theta_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}=(\zeta,\mathsf{acv}_{0},\mathsf{arb}_{0},b)
denote the optimal sample sizes for
\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
as a function of
\theta_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}
that also satisfy
\mathsf{ACV}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}(n_{1}^{{\text{opt}}},n_{2}^{{\text{opt}}}|\theta_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}})=\mathsf{acv}_{0}
and
\mathsf{ARB}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}=\mathsf{arb}_{0}\zeta^{b}
for
\mathsf{acv}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
\mathsf{arb}_{0}\in(0,1)
, and
b\in\mathbb{R}_{0}^{+}
fixed. Then, for
\zeta\in\{0,\zeta_{b,1}\}
:

(n_{1}^{{\text{opt}}},n_{2}^{{\text{opt}}})(\theta_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}})=(2\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{-2}+5,1).

Likewise, for all remaining
\zeta\in[0,1]\setminus\{0,\zeta_{b,1}\}
:

(n_{1}^{{\text{opt}}},n_{2}^{{\text{opt}}})(\theta_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}})=(2\alpha_{1}^{{\text{opt}}}+1,2\alpha_{2}^{{\text{opt}}}+1)(\theta_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}),

where
\alpha_{1}^{{\text{opt}}}
and
\alpha_{2}^{{\text{opt}}}
satisfy

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha_{2}}\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}(\mathrm{A}-\alpha_{2},\alpha_{2}|\theta_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}})\Big|_{(\mathrm{A},\alpha_{2})=(\alpha_{1}^{\text{opt}}+\alpha_{2}^{\text{opt}},\alpha_{2}^{\text{opt}})}

\displaystyle=0

(3.14)

\displaystyle\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}(\alpha_{1}^{\text{opt}},\alpha_{2}^{\text{opt}}|\theta_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}})-\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{2}

\displaystyle=0.

Proof.

We begin with the special case
\zeta\in\{0,\zeta_{b,1}\}
and call upon Corollary
16
and Lemma
34
to find

\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}|\theta_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}})=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{(1)_{k}(1)_{k}}{(\alpha_{1})_{k}k!}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}-2}.

Since
\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
is independent of
\alpha_{2}
it follows that the optimization problem (
3.14
) is weakly degenerate so that
\alpha_{2}^{\text{opt}}=0
and
\alpha_{1}^{\text{opt}}
satisfies

\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}^{\text{opt}}-2}=\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{2}.

Solving for
\alpha_{1}^{\text{opt}}
and then using the relation
n_{i}^{\text{opt}}=2n_{i}^{\text{opt}}+1
then gives the desired result for the special case
\zeta\in\{0,\zeta_{b,1}\}
.

Now consider the complementary case
\zeta\in[0,1]\setminus\{0,\zeta_{b,1}\}
. Upon inspection of the double integral representation of
\mathsf{E}\mathscr{T}_{\nu}^{2}
in Theorem
9
and assuming appropriate values of
(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})
to guarantee convergence, we see that
\mathsf{E}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}^{2}(\mathrm{A}-\alpha_{2},\alpha_{2}|\theta_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}})
is a smooth function of
\alpha_{2}
and hence so is
\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}(\mathrm{A}-\alpha_{2},\alpha_{2}|\theta_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}})
. Additionally, upon writing

\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu}(\mathrm{A}-\alpha_{2},\alpha_{2}|\theta_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}})=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}f_{k}(\alpha_{2}|\mathrm{A},\theta_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}),

we conclude from Lemma
35
that
\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu}(\mathrm{A}-\alpha_{2},\alpha_{2}|\theta_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}})
is:
1)
positive and strictly convex on
\alpha_{2}\in(0,\mathrm{A})
and
2)
infinite at the endpoints
\alpha_{2}=0,\mathrm{A}
. As such, it follows that for every set of parameters
\theta_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}
with
\zeta\notin\{0,\zeta_{b,1}\}
that
\alpha_{2}^{\text{opt}}(\mathrm{A},\theta_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}})=\arginf_{\alpha_{2}\in(0,\mathrm{A})}\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu}(\mathrm{A}-\alpha_{2},\alpha_{2}|\theta_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}})
is unique and corresponds to

\partial_{\alpha_{2}}\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu}(\mathrm{A}-\alpha_{2},\alpha_{2}|\theta_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}})_{\alpha_{2}=\alpha_{2}^{\text{opt}}}=0.

This is turn implies the optimization problem for finding
(\alpha_{1}^{\text{opt}},\alpha_{2}^{\text{opt}})
is nondegenerate and is given by (
3.14
). The proof is now complete.
∎

Taking a small detour, we now relate the optimal sample sizes for
\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
in Theorem
18
back to a classical result given by James Janesick in his book
Photon Transfer
.

Remark 3

(Optimal sample sizes in the shot noise limit)
.

Using the expression for
\mathscr{E}_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}
in Corollary
15
as well as the results from Theorem
18
it is straightforward to deduce

\lim_{\zeta\nearrow 0}\mathscr{E}_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}=1,

which shows that the optimal sample sizes for
\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
equal those for
\mathscr{G}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
in the shot noise limit. If one wants to obtain a
1\%
relative uncertainty for the measurement of
g
in the shot noise limit, i.e.
\lim_{\zeta\nearrow 0}\mathsf{ACV}\mathscr{G}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}=0.01
, then according to Theorem
18
the optimal total number of samples needed is
\mathrm{N}^{\text{opt}}=20,006
. This exact result agrees with Janesick’s estimate
\mathrm{N}^{\text{opt}}\approx 20,000
of
[
16
, pgs. 79–81]
.

Up to this point we have made a significant amount of progress in studying the optimal sample sizes of
\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
. In particular, we have established uniqueness of these optimal sample sizes, derived their exact expressions at the two points of interest
\zeta\in\{0,\zeta_{b,1}\}
, determined a system of equations for evaluating them at all remaining
\zeta\in[0,1]\setminus\{0,\zeta_{b,1}\}
, studied their asymptotic behavior in the low illumination limit
\zeta\searrow 1
, and derived the expression
\mathscr{E}_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}
, which allow us to determine when they give a good approximation to the optimal sample sizes of
\mathscr{G}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
. With so much theoretical understanding of these optimal sample sizes we’re in a good place to begin discussing how we actually compute them.

It is of no surprise that the system of equations for evaluating the optimal sample sizes given in (
3.14
) lacks a closed-from solution and so we must turn to numerical methods to solve it. This of course introduces the practical problem of implementing root finding algorithms. We already know the bounds of the parameters
\theta_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}
and so the only thing left to implement (
3.14
) is a good approximation for
(n_{1}^{\text{opt}},n_{2}^{\text{opt}})
to use as a starting point. Because
\mathsf{ACV}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
is naturally parameterized in terms of
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
we will seek an approximate solution for the optimal pair
(\alpha_{1}^{\text{opt}},\alpha_{2}^{\text{opt}})
and then transform it into
(n_{1}^{\text{opt}},n_{2}^{\text{opt}})
via the relation
n_{i}^{\text{opt}}=2\alpha_{i}^{\text{opt}}+1
.

To begin deriving our approximation we call on the series expansion given in Corollary
12
to write
\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}|\theta_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}})\approx\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}^{\ast}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}|\theta_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}})
, where

\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}^{\ast}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}|\theta_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}})=\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}}\tilde{g}_{1,1}^{2}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})+\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}\tilde{g}_{2,1}^{2}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})+\frac{1}{\alpha_{2}}\tilde{g}_{1,0}^{2}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})

(3.15)

and
\tilde{g}_{k,\ell}(z,\nu)
given by Corollary
10

(\mathrm{i})
. Upon substituting
\alpha_{1}=\mathrm{A}-\alpha_{2}
into this approximation we see that it possesses the same useful properties of the exact form of
\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}(\mathrm{A}-\alpha_{2},\alpha_{2}|\theta_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}})
, namely, positivity, smoothness, strictly convexity on
\alpha_{2}\in(0,\mathrm{A})
, as well as the same limiting properties at the endpoints
\alpha_{2}=0,\mathrm{A}
. For the sake of brevity we let
a=\tilde{g}_{1,1}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})
,
b=\tilde{g}_{2,1}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})
,
c=\tilde{g}_{1,0}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})
, and
d=\mathsf{acv}_{0}
and then substitute
\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}^{\ast}
into (
3.14
) yielding the system of equations

\displaystyle(1)

\displaystyle\frac{a^{2}}{\alpha_{1}^{2}}+\frac{b^{2}}{\alpha_{1}^{2}\alpha_{2}}-\frac{b^{2}}{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}^{2}}-\frac{c^{2}}{\alpha_{2}^{2}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle(2)

\displaystyle\frac{a^{2}}{\alpha_{1}}+\frac{b^{2}}{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}+\frac{c^{2}}{\alpha_{2}}-d^{2}

\displaystyle=0.

To put this system into a more useful form we perform the transformations
(1)^{\prime}=(2)/\alpha_{1}-(1)
and
(2)^{\prime}=(2)/\alpha_{2}+(1)
to get the equivalent set of equations

\displaystyle(1)^{\prime}

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c^{2}}\left(d^{2}\alpha_{2}^{2}-c^{2}\alpha_{2}-b^{2}\right)

\displaystyle(2)^{\prime}

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{a^{2}}\left(d^{2}\alpha_{1}^{2}-a^{2}\alpha_{1}-b^{2}\right).

Now substituting the r.h.s. of
(1)^{\prime}
into
(2)^{\prime}
and the r.h.s. of
(2)^{\prime}
into
(1)^{\prime}
separates the variables
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
into two quartic equations, each of which yields four roots for a total of sixteen possible solutions to our problem. Substituting all sixteen potential solutions back into our original system we find only two work, namely,

(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=\left(\frac{a^{2}\pm\sqrt{a^{2}c^{2}+b^{2}d^{2}}}{d^{2}},\frac{c^{2}\pm\sqrt{a^{2}c^{2}+b^{2}d^{2}}}{d^{2}}\right).

(3.16)

To obtain the final solution we must determine which sign in front of the root to take. Taking the positive sign clearly yields a nonnegative solution for
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
so to verify this is the correct choice we must show that choosing the negative sign yields a nonsensical solution. The following two lemmas show that if we choose the negative sign then the solution for
\alpha_{2}
is nonpositive and thus is not the desired choice. Since the choice of sign for both
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
must be the same it follows that the positive sign is indeed the correct choice.

Lemma 36
.

Let
{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
be as defined in Definition
13
. Then for all
\zeta\in(0,1)
:
\tilde{g}_{1,0}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})\geq-\frac{1}{2}
.

Proof.

We begin with the explicit form

\tilde{g}_{1,0}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})=\frac{\zeta}{1-\zeta}-\frac{(\log_{\zeta}\mathsf{arb}_{0}+b)\mathsf{arb}_{0}\zeta^{b}}{1-\mathsf{arb}_{0}\zeta^{b}}.

Our approach will be to put a lower bound on
\tilde{g}_{1,0}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})
by successively minimizing it w.r.t. each of its variables. Differentiating w.r.t.
b
we find

\partial_{b}\tilde{g}_{1,0}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})=\frac{x(x-\log x-1)}{(1-x)^{2}}\Big|_{x=\mathsf{arb}_{0}\zeta^{b}}.

For the specified parameter restriction we have
0<\mathsf{arb}_{0}\zeta^{b}<\mathsf{arb}_{0}<1\implies x\in(0,1)
; thus, it is straightforward to show that the above expression in
x
is positive. It follows that

\tilde{g}_{1,0}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})\geq\tilde{g}_{1,0}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}_{b=0})=\frac{\zeta}{1-\zeta}-\frac{(\log_{\zeta}\mathsf{arb}_{0})\mathsf{arb}_{0}}{1-\mathsf{arb}_{0}}.

Now differentiating w.r.t.
\mathsf{arb}_{0}
we have

\partial_{\mathsf{arb}_{0}}\tilde{g}_{1,0}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}_{b=0})=\frac{1}{\log\zeta}\frac{\mathsf{arb}_{0}-\log\mathsf{arb}_{0}-1}{(1-\mathsf{arb}_{0})^{2}}.

The quantity involving
\mathsf{arb}_{0}
is positive on
\mathsf{arb}_{0}\in(0,1)
while
\log\zeta<0
on
\zeta\in(0,1)
. Therefore,
\partial_{\mathsf{arb}_{0}}\tilde{g}_{1,0}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}_{b=0})<0
and

\tilde{g}_{1,0}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}_{b=0})\geq\lim_{\mathsf{arb}_{0}\searrow 1}\tilde{g}_{1,0}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}_{b=0})=\frac{\zeta}{1-\zeta}+\frac{1}{\log\zeta}.

The resulting function of
\zeta
is strictly decreasing in
\zeta
. Taking the limit
\zeta\searrow 1
subsequently gives

\tilde{g}_{1,0}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}_{b=0})\geq\lim_{\zeta\searrow 1}\frac{\zeta}{1-\zeta}+\frac{1}{\log\zeta}=-\frac{1}{2}.

The proof is now complete.
∎

Lemma 37
.

Choosing the negative sign in
(\ref{eq:aprx_system_potential_solutions})
leads to
\alpha_{2}\leq 0
.

Proof.

For brevity we will use
\tilde{g}_{n,\omega}
to denote
\tilde{g}_{n,\omega}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})
. We need to show

\tilde{g}_{1,0}^{2}-\sqrt{\tilde{g}_{1,0}^{2}\tilde{g}_{1,1}^{2}+\tilde{g}_{2,0}^{2}\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{2}}\leq 0.

Since all quantities under the radical are positive it follows that

\tilde{g}_{1,0}^{2}-\sqrt{\tilde{g}_{1,0}^{2}\tilde{g}_{1,1}^{2}+\tilde{g}_{2,0}^{2}\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{2}}\leq\tilde{g}_{1,0}^{2}-\sqrt{\tilde{g}_{1,0}^{2}\tilde{g}_{1,1}^{2}}.

From Lemma
13
we have
\tilde{g}_{1,1}=\tilde{g}_{1,0}+1
; hence,

\tilde{g}_{1,0}^{2}-\sqrt{\tilde{g}_{1,0}^{2}\tilde{g}_{1,1}^{2}+\tilde{g}_{2,0}^{2}\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{2}}\leq\tilde{g}_{1,0}^{2}-\sqrt{\tilde{g}_{1,0}^{4}+\tilde{g}_{1,0}^{2}(2\tilde{g}_{1,0}+1)}.

But now recall from Lemma
36
that
\tilde{g}_{1,0}\geq-\frac{1}{2}\implies\tilde{g}_{1,0}^{2}(2\tilde{g}_{1,0}+1)\geq 0
. So it follows that

\tilde{g}_{1,0}^{2}-\sqrt{\tilde{g}_{1,0}^{2}\tilde{g}_{1,1}^{2}+\tilde{g}_{2,0}^{2}\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{2}}\leq\tilde{g}_{1,0}^{2}-\sqrt{\tilde{g}_{1,0}^{4}}=0,

which completes the proof.
∎

Now knowing that we must choose the positive sign in
(\ref{eq:aprx_system_potential_solutions})
we have after reintroducing
a
,
b
,
c
,
d
, and
n_{i}=2\alpha_{i}+1
:

\displaystyle n_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{2}}\left(\tilde{g}_{1,1}^{2}+\sqrt{\tilde{g}_{1,1}^{2}\tilde{g}_{1,0}^{2}+\tilde{g}_{2,1}^{2}\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{2}}\right)+1

\displaystyle n_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{2}}\left(\tilde{g}_{1,0}^{2}+\sqrt{\tilde{g}_{1,1}^{2}\tilde{g}_{1,0}^{2}+\tilde{g}_{2,1}^{2}\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{2}}\right)+1,

which is the desired solution for the approximate optimal sample sizes of
\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
. So how good are these approximations? First note that with the results of Corollary
16
and Lemma
34
we have

\lim_{\zeta\nearrow L}(n_{1},n_{2})(\theta_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}})=(2\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{-2}+1,1),\quad L\in\{0,\zeta_{b,1}\}.

Comparing this with Theorem
18
we see that our approximate solutions can be made exact at
\zeta=\{0,\zeta_{b,1}\}
by instead using the slightly modified solution

\displaystyle n_{1}^{\ast}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{2}}\left(\tilde{g}_{1,1}^{2}+\sqrt{\tilde{g}_{1,1}^{2}\tilde{g}_{1,0}^{2}+\tilde{g}_{2,1}^{2}\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{2}}\right)+5

(3.17)

\displaystyle n_{2}^{\ast}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{2}}\left(\tilde{g}_{1,0}^{2}+\sqrt{\tilde{g}_{1,1}^{2}\tilde{g}_{1,0}^{2}+\tilde{g}_{2,1}^{2}\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{2}}\right)+1.

At the opposite end of the
\zeta
-domain, we may use Lemma
30
to deduce as
\zeta\searrow 1
:
n_{2}^{\ast}/n_{1}^{\ast}\to 1
and
n_{i}^{\ast}\sim C_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}^{\ast}(1-\zeta)^{-2}
, where

C_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}^{\ast}=\frac{2\gamma^{2}(2,-\log\mathsf{arb}_{0})}{\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{2}(1-\mathsf{arb}_{0})^{2}}\left(1+\left(1+\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{2}(1-\mathsf{arb}_{0})^{2}\frac{\gamma^{2}(3,-\log\mathsf{arb}_{0})}{\gamma^{4}(2,-\log\mathsf{arb}_{0})}\right)^{1/2}\right).

Table
3.4
tabulates the ratio
C_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}^{\ast}/C_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}
for a variety of values for
\mathsf{arb}_{0}
and
\mathsf{acv}_{0}
. One can see that the ratio is near unity for these values which indicates our approximate solutions for
(n_{1}^{\text{opt}},n_{2}^{\text{opt}})
given by (
3.17
) are not only exact at
\zeta=\{0,\zeta_{b,1}\}
but are also exceptionally accurate for small
\mathsf{arb}_{0}
and
\mathsf{acv}_{0}
in the neighborhood of
\zeta=1
.

With a suitable approximation to the optimal sample sizes we may finally turn to implementing a numerical routine for computing them. Numerically solving the system (
3.14
) ultimately requires a method for computing
\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
and its partial derivative w.r.t.
\alpha_{2}
. The fact that the approximation for
\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
in (
3.15
) lead to excellent approximations for the optimal sample sizes across the entire
\zeta
-domain tells us that we may compute the optimal sample sizes by substituting

\mathsf{ACV}_{n}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}\frac{\tilde{g}_{k,\ell}^{2}(\zeta,{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})}{(\alpha_{1})_{\ell}(\alpha_{2})_{k-\ell}\ell!(k-\ell)!}.

(3.18)

into (
3.14
) for some appropriate value of
n
. To determine an appropriate value for
n
we consider the absolute relative error

R_{n,2}=\left\lvert\frac{\mathsf{ACV}_{n}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}{\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}-1\right\rvert,

which according to Corollary
17
is bounded above by
R_{n,2}\leq R_{n,m,2}^{\ast}
with

R_{n,m,2}^{\ast}=\left\lvert\left(1+\frac{E_{n,m}^{\ast}}{\mathsf{ACV}_{n}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}\right)^{-1}-1\right\rvert

and
E_{n,m}^{\ast}
given in Theorem
20
. Before proceeding, the following result gives us an exact value for
R_{n,2}
at
\zeta\in\{0,\zeta_{b,1}\}
.

Lemma 38
.

For
\zeta\in\{0,\zeta_{b,1}\}

R_{n,2}|_{(n_{1},n_{2})=(n_{1}^{\text{opt}},n_{2}^{\text{opt}})}=\frac{(n+1)!}{(\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{-2}+2)_{n}}.

Proof.

The proof follows from noting that at
\zeta=0,\zeta_{b,1}
:

\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}-2},

\mathsf{ACV}_{n}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}-2}\left(1-\frac{(n+1)!}{(\alpha_{1})_{n}}\right),

and
\alpha_{1}^{\text{opt}}=\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{-2}+2
.
∎

The sharpness of the upper bound
R_{n,m,2}^{\ast}
improves with increasing
m
but the rate at which this happens significantly decreases for
m>n+1
; thus, it seems reasonable to use
R_{n,n+1}^{\ast}
as the upper bound in our numerical routine. To get a sense of how
R_{n,n+1,2}^{\ast}
behaves, we evaluated it at
(n_{1},n_{2})=(n_{1}^{\ast},n_{2}^{\ast})
for
\mathsf{arb}_{0}=0.5
,
\mathsf{acv}_{0}=0.05
, and various values of
b
and
n
as plotted in Figure
3.2
. We observe that as
n
increases, the upper bound decreases rapidly except for values of
\zeta
in the neighborhood of
\zeta=0,\zeta_{b,1},1
. However, notice that for the exact value of
R_{n,2}
given by Lemma
38
:
R_{1,2}=4.98\times 10^{-3}
,
R_{3,2}=3.67\times 10^{-7}
,
R_{5,2}=6.69\times 10^{-11}
,
R_{7,2}=2.26\times 10^{-14}
. Since the approximations
n_{1}^{\ast}
and
n_{2}^{\ast}
are exact as
\zeta\to L
,
L\in\{0,\zeta_{b,1}\}
, we can conclude that the high upper bound for
R_{n,2}
near these values of
\zeta
is the result of a deficiency of
R_{n,n+1,2}^{\ast}
and not that the relative error isn’t decrease rapidly with
n
. As such, estimates for the appropriate value of
n
in (
3.18
) determined using
R_{n,n+1,2}^{\ast}
will be, in some cases, significantly overestimated near these points.

Figure 3.2:
Absolute relative error bound
R_{n,n+1,2}^{\ast}
versus
\zeta
for
\mathsf{arb}_{0}=0.5
,
\mathsf{acv}_{0}=0.05
,
b=0,1
, and
n=1,3,5,7
.

Given these observations, Algorithm
1
presents a simple procedure for computing the optimal sample sizes for
\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
.

Algorithm 1

Optimal sample size pseudo-code.

1:

procedure

OptimalSamples
(
\zeta
,
\mathsf{arb}_{0}
,
b
,
\mathsf{acv}_{0}
,
\epsilon
,
n_{\max}
)

2:

if

\zeta\in\{0,\zeta_{b,1}\}

then

3:

(n_{1}^{\text{opt}},n_{2}^{\text{opt}})=(2\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{-2}+5,1)
;

4:

return

(n_{1}^{\text{opt}},n_{2}^{\text{opt}})
;

5:

else

6:

n=1
;

7:

while

R_{n,n+1,2}^{\ast}(\zeta,n_{1}^{\ast},n_{2}^{\ast})>\epsilon

and

n<n_{\max}

do

8:

n=n+1
;

9:

Substitute
\mathsf{ACV}_{n}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
in (
3.14
);

10:

Solve for
(n_{1}^{\text{opt}},n_{2}^{\text{opt}})
using starting point
(n_{1}^{\ast},n_{2}^{\ast})
;

11:

if

R_{n,n+1,2}^{\ast}(\zeta,n_{1}^{\text{opt}},n_{2}^{\text{opt}})\leq\epsilon

or

n=n_{\max}

then

12:

return

(n_{1}^{\text{opt}},n_{2}^{\text{opt}})
;

13:

14:

15:

;

To demonstrate its effectiveness, Algorithm
1
was implemented in
mathematica
using the
FindRoot[]
function for Line
10
to numerically solve the system (
3.14
). The procedure was executed for
\zeta\in[0,1)
,
\mathsf{arb}_{0}=0.5
,
\mathsf{acv}_{0}=0.05
, and four different values of
b
ranging from zero to one. Figure
3.3
plots the computed sample sizes along with
\mathscr{E}_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}
evaluated at a dark noise of
\sigma_{\mathrm{d}}g=1e\mathchar 45\relax
. The values chosen for
\mathsf{arb}_{0}
and
\sigma_{\mathrm{d}}g
, while not typical, were chosen to exaggerate the features of the optimal sample size curves discussed in the preceding analysis.

Focusing first on the curve for
n_{2}^{\text{opt}}
we observe for values of
b<1
that the curve has a cusp at
\zeta=0
and
\zeta=\zeta_{b,1}
where it takes on a value of one. As,
\zeta\searrow 1
this curve approaches the
n_{1}^{\text{opt}}
curve; both of which approach
C_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}(1-\zeta)^{-2}
in the limit. As for the
\mathscr{E}_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}
curve, we see that it equals one at
\zeta=0
and
\overline{\mathscr{E}_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}}=0.15847\dots
at
\zeta=1
. Furthermore, the cusp present in the
n_{2}^{\text{opt}}
curve is also present in the curve for
\mathscr{E}_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}
, which highlights the reason why choosing values of
b
less than one may yield undesirable results, in particular, unacceptably low values of
\mathscr{E}_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}
.

As for the computational details of the routine, it was decided to use
\epsilon=5\times 10^{-7}
so that
\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
was approximated to at least seven significant digits and
n_{\max}=20
to avoid issues of numerical stability. Figure
3.4
plots the number of terms,
n
, and upper bound on the absolute relative error,
R_{n,n+1,2}^{\ast}
, as a function of
\zeta
obtained from the routine for the case
b=0
. From the figure we observe that
R_{n,n+1,2}^{\ast}
exhibits a sharp increase near the points
\zeta\in\{0,\zeta_{b,1}\}
; resulting in larger values for
n
. Only a few
\zeta
-values near
\zeta_{b,1}
hit the bound
n=20
, which is the same behavior observed for all other values of
b
in Figure
3.3
. To check the quality of the solution at these problematic points we note that in the neighborhood of
\zeta=\zeta_{b,1}
that
{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
and
n_{2}^{\text{opt}}
are quite small while
n_{1}^{\text{opt}}
is large. Recalling the double integral representation of
\mathsf{E}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}^{2}
we may then approximate
\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
with

\mathsf{ACV}_{n}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{(1+{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}})_{k}^{2}}{(\alpha_{1})_{k}k!}\frac{\left((1-x)(1-y)\right)^{k}}{\left((1-(1-\zeta)x)(1-(1-\zeta)y)\right)^{k}}\\
\times{F}\left({1-{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}},1-{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}\atop\alpha_{2}};(1-x)(1-y)\right)f_{XY}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y-1,

(3.19)

where

f_{XY}(x,y)=\frac{\left((1-(1-\zeta)x)(1-(1-\zeta)y)\right)^{-{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}-1}}{F(1,1+{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}};2;1-\zeta)^{2}}

and the error
E_{n}
such that
\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}=\mathsf{ACV}_{n}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}+E_{n}
is positive and bounded above by

E_{n}^{\ast}={F}\left({1+{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}},1+{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}\atop\alpha_{1}};1\right)_{\!\!n+1}^{\!c}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\left((1-x)(1-y)\right)^{n+1}}{\left((1-(1-\zeta)x)(1-(1-\zeta)y)\right)^{n+1}}\\
\times{F}\left({1-{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}},1-{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}\atop\alpha_{2}};(1-x)(1-y)\right)f_{XY}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

with
F(\alpha,\beta;\gamma;z)_{n}^{c}
given in Proposition
9
. Looking back as the numerical data we observe that one of the problematic points in Figure
3.4
occurs at
\zeta=0.499443
. Using this value for
\zeta
and the optimal sample sizes computed from our routine we find

\left\lvert\frac{\mathsf{ACV}_{20}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}{\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}}-1\right\rvert\leq 6.21\times 10^{-15},

which shows that the approximation
\mathsf{ACV}_{20}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
given by (
3.19
) approximates the exact value
\mathsf{ACV}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
to approximately fourteen significant digits at this point of interest. Substituting
\mathsf{ACV}_{20}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}
into (
3.14
) we then find

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha_{2}}\mathsf{ACV}_{20}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}(\mathrm{A}-\alpha_{2},\alpha_{2}|\theta_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}})\Big|_{(\mathrm{A},\alpha_{2})=(\alpha_{1}^{\text{opt}}+\alpha_{2}^{\text{opt}},\alpha_{2}^{\text{opt}})}

\displaystyle=-6.84\times 10^{-9}

\displaystyle\mathsf{ACV}_{20}^{2}\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}(\alpha_{1}^{\text{opt}},\alpha_{2}^{\text{opt}}|\theta_{\mathscr{T}_{\nu^{\smash{\dagger}}}})-\mathsf{acv}_{0}^{2}

\displaystyle=9.48\times 10^{-10},

which shows no significant deviation from the desired solution
(0,0)
. This confirms that the solution to the optimal sample sizes obtained in our routine near
\zeta=\zeta_{b,1}
are not in significant error as the upper bound
R_{n,n+1,2}^{\ast}
might suggest.
